I wouldn’t say there’s a clear link, like something sexual happens and then I have one of those eating episodes afterward, or that the eating is tied to that in some direct way.\n\nI guess I’d want to be sure I understand what you mean by “associated with,” because if you mean whether they happen around the same time or because of it, I haven’t seen that pattern. It’s not one of the things I’ve thought, “Oh, that’s what set it off.” Mostly I’ve been looking for other kinds of patterns, and even those I haven’t been able to pin down very well.\n\nSo my honest answer is: not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed any unusual sexual thoughts or feelings during these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I have. I mean, I’m trying to be careful, because that’s a pretty specific question and I don’t want to just dismiss it without thinking. But when I picture what’s going on during those episodes, it’s mostly about the food and this kind of urgency around eating, and then all the mental noise about what I’m doing. I don’t remember sexual thoughts being part of it.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s any unusual feeling like that that comes over me during it, at least not that I’ve noticed. If anything, I’m usually pretty focused on the eating itself and not really tuned into anything else in that way.\n\nSo… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difference in how often these eating episodes happen when you are in a sexually active relationship versus when you are not?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure that I’ve noticed a difference, honestly. I’m trying to think back over times when I was in a relationship versus when I wasn’t, and I can’t really say there’s a clear pattern there.\n\nIt’s not like I can look at it and say, “Oh, when I’m sexually active it happens more,” or “when I’m not, it happens less.” I don’t think I’ve tracked it that way in my head. And because the episodes themselves already feel kind of hard to predict, it’s difficult for me to separate that out from everything else that might be going on.\n\nSo… no, I haven’t noticed a meaningful difference, at least not one I feel confident saying is real.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel sexually aroused while you are having one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’m trying to separate out what you mean by aroused versus just, I don’t know, worked up or agitated, because during those episodes there can be a lot of intensity in general. But I wouldn’t describe it as sexual arousal.\n\nWhen it’s happening, my attention is really on the eating and the feeling of not quite being able to slow it down. I’m not thinking about sex, and I don’t remember my body reacting in that kind of way either. It’s more like I’m caught up in the episode itself, not anything sexual.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed feeling sexually aroused during them.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating episodes help you feel better or calm down in any way after they happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because “better” is… complicated. In the moment, I guess there can be this feeling like I’m doing something that takes over everything else, so maybe it shuts things out for a bit. But after it happens, I wouldn’t really say I feel calm in a clean or lasting way.\n\nIt’s more like there’s a shift, maybe a kind of temporary quiet, but I don’t know if I’d call that feeling better. Sometimes I’m just sort of worn out afterward, and it’s hard to tell whether that’s calm or just being depleted. I haven’t really sat down and measured it like, “Was I calmer afterward?” so I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nSo… maybe briefly, in the sense that it interrupts whatever was going on in my head, but I’m not sure I’d say it truly helps.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating episodes are a way for you to deal with or escape from difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that clearly. I mean, I understand why you’re asking, because from the outside it probably looks like it could be a way of dealing with feelings or shutting something off. And maybe sometimes it does sort of pull my attention away from whatever else is going on, but I’m hesitant to say, “Yes, that’s what it is,” because I don’t always have a neat emotion beforehand that I can point to.\n\nSometimes I’m aware I’m tense or bothered or just kind of unsettled, but other times it feels more automatic than that. Like I’m not sitting there thinking, “I can’t handle this feeling, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more muddled. The episode starts, and then everything narrows down to that, and only afterward do I try to figure out what was going on.\n\nSo… I guess it might function that way at times, as an escape or distraction, but I’m not fully sure. I haven’t noticed a consistent enough pattern to say it’s definitely about difficult emotions every time.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating episodes are triggered by or help you cope with negative emotions?\n\nPatient: I know I’ve sort of answered this already, but I’m still not completely sure. I can see how negative emotions might be part of it sometimes, because if I’m irritated or anxious or just feeling low, the eating can kind of take over my attention for a while. It’s like everything narrows down to the food and the episode itself, and whatever else was bothering me gets pushed to the side.\n\nBut I don’t want to say it’s always triggered by negative feelings, because I haven’t noticed it that cleanly. Sometimes I don’t know what I was feeling right before it started, or it feels more like I’m already halfway into it before I’ve even identified any emotion. Afterward I might look back and think, “Maybe I was stressed,” but that’s not the same as knowing it in the moment.\n\nSo… maybe it does help me cope in a temporary, not very good way sometimes, but I can’t really say I’ve figured out a definite pattern.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the eating episodes are a way for you to regulate or manage your emotions in some way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I keep circling around that idea because it sounds like it might fit, but I don’t know if I experience it in such a clear way. “Regulate emotions” sounds very organized, like there’s a feeling, and then I do this thing to bring it down or control it. For me, it doesn’t feel that deliberate.\n\nWhat I can say is that when it’s happening, my focus shifts pretty completely. Whatever I was thinking about before kind of gets crowded out. So in that sense, maybe it changes what I’m feeling, or at least blocks it for a while. But I don’t always know what emotion I’m supposedly managing. Sometimes I can look back afterward and think maybe I was stressed or uncomfortable or frustrated, but in the moment it’s not like I’ve labeled it.\n\nSo I guess… maybe it functions that way sometimes, but I can’t say I’ve really identified it as an emotion-management thing. It feels more messy and automatic than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes have anything to do with sexual feelings or desires?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean… that’s not something I’ve connected it to, at least not in any clear way. When these episodes happen, I’m not aware of sexual feelings or desires being part of it, or leading into it, or anything like that.\n\nI suppose I can’t say I’ve analyzed every single time perfectly, because sometimes I don’t even have a good handle on what I’m feeling beforehand in general. But if you’re asking whether it feels sexually driven, or tied to urges like that, no, that doesn’t really fit how I experience it.\n\nIt feels more like the food and the eating become the whole focus. Not sexual, not romantic, not anything along those lines. I haven’t noticed a connection there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the eating episodes are more likely to happen when you're feeling emotionally overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that confidently. I mean, “emotionally overwhelmed” is one of those phrases where part of me thinks, yes, maybe, because there are times when I’m tense or wound up or I feel like there’s too much in my head, and then the eating episode seems to just… happen, or I get pulled into it.\n\nBut I don’t have a clean enough pattern to say, “That’s when it happens.” Sometimes I don’t even recognize that I’m overwhelmed until afterward, if that makes sense. In the moment, I might just feel restless or uncomfortable or like I want something, and I don’t necessarily connect it to an emotion right away. Then later I’ll think, well, maybe I was more upset or stressed than I realized.\n\nSo I’d say it could be more likely sometimes, but I haven’t really tracked it well enough to know. It doesn’t feel like a simple cause-and-effect thing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what and how much you eat during normal meals, outside of these episodes?\n\nPatient: During normal meals, I think it’s more or less okay. Like if I’m having dinner in the usual way, sitting down with a plate, I can usually decide what I’m eating and stop when the meal is done. It doesn’t feel completely out of my hands in that setting.\n\nWhere it gets different is later, especially at night. That’s really the part that bothers me and is kind of why I’m here. Once I start eating then, it’s like I’m watching myself do it. I can be thinking very clearly, “You should stop now,” or “You don’t need any more,” and at the same time my hands are still going back for more food. It’s not even like I’m unaware of it. I’m aware, which almost makes it more frustrating, because I’m seeing it happen and I’m telling myself to stop, but I don’t seem to actually stop.\n\nSo with normal meals, yes, I’d say I have some control. But outside that, once the nighttime eating starts, it feels like the control just drops off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you lose control over eating specifically because of the type of food you're consuming?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s because of the type of food exactly. I mean, certain foods are easier to keep reaching for, sure, but that’s not really how it feels to me at the time. It’s not like I eat one specific thing and that food somehow flips a switch.\n\nWhat brings me in is more that once I start eating at night, I can sort of see myself doing it. I’ll be standing there or sitting there, and I’m thinking, very clearly, “Okay, stop now. That’s enough. Put it away.” And then my hands just keep going back for more anyway. It’s a strange feeling, because I’m not blacked out or unaware. I know what I’m doing while I’m doing it, and I still don’t seem to stop.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s specifically the food itself. It’s more the starting, especially at night, and then it gets away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the eating episodes are usually preceded by a specific emotional state, such as anxiety or sadness?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can pin it down that neatly. I know you mentioned anxiety or sadness, and I wouldn’t want to say, yes, it’s always anxiety, or yes, it’s sadness, because that doesn’t feel accurate to me. Sometimes before it happens I’m just sort of keyed up or unsettled, but I don’t always know what to call it.\n\nIt’s not like I sit there thinking, “I’m sad, so now I’m going to eat,” or “I’m anxious, so this is happening.” A lot of the time I don’t recognize much beforehand except maybe restlessness, or being tired, or just having that uncomfortable feeling where I want to get away from whatever is in my head. Then afterward I might look back and wonder if I was stressed or upset and just didn’t admit it to myself at the time.\n\nSo I guess the honest answer is I haven’t noticed one specific emotional state that reliably comes before it. There may be feelings there, but I don’t identify them clearly enough in the moment to say it’s usually one thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes could be related to any sexual issues or concerns you might have?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. When you say sexual issues, I’m not completely sure what sort of thing you mean—like relationship problems, performance worries, past experiences, or just thoughts and feelings around sex generally?\n\nI haven’t connected the eating episodes to that, at least not consciously. It’s not something where I have a sexual concern and then I go eat, or where those thoughts are obviously there beforehand. I suppose there are personal things people don’t always think about directly, and maybe there are connections I’m not seeing, but I wouldn’t want to make one up just to answer the question.\n\nFrom what I can tell, it doesn’t feel sexually driven or tied to a specific sexual concern. If there’s something more specific you’re asking about, I can try to answer that, but on my own I haven’t noticed a link.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other psychological issues, such as depression or anxiety disorders, alongside these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I haven’t been told I have something like that, if that’s what you mean, and I don’t want to start diagnosing myself. I mean, I have days where I feel worn down or worried, like anyone does, and this whole eating thing definitely makes me feel worse afterward, but I can’t say I’ve recognized a separate depression or anxiety problem alongside it.\n\nSometimes I wonder if there’s more going on emotionally than I’m admitting to myself, because the episodes don’t happen in a vacuum exactly. But if you’re asking whether I’ve had a clear, ongoing anxiety disorder or depression that I know about, I don’t really have that information. I haven’t noticed it in a way where I could say, yes, that’s definitely part of it."
